    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F45.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain without sounding foolish, I suppose. I mean, I'm 62 years old, I've worked the land my whole life, been out in the sun, the wind, the dirt. You expect some wear and tear, right? But lately, I just... I can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. It's not just a passing thought, it's like this heavy thing sitting on my chest all day. I look in the mirror and all I see are these deep, jagged cracks in my skin, these dark sun spots that seem to be multiplying, and the way my cheeks are just sagging down. It's like my face is melting off my skull. \n\nAnd it's not just when I'm at home. When I have to go into town, say, to the bank to talk about the farm loan, or to the county office for some paperwork... I can feel them staring. I know they're looking right at my hands when I sign a paper, looking at the dirt that never seems to wash out of the creases, looking at how leathery and awful I look. I feel like they're judging me, thinking I'm some sort of... I don't know, some sort of monster. My wife, she tells me I'm crazy. She says, \"Frank, you just look like a man who's worked outdoors his whole life, there's nothing wrong with you.\" But she doesn't see what I see. She doesn't feel the eyes on her when she walks into a room. It's gotten to the point where I don't even want to go into town anymore, I just want to stay out in the fields where nobody can see me. It's exhausting, doc, it really is. I just can't stop thinking about how terrible I look.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend thinking about your appearance on a typical day?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a tough one to put a number on, you know? It's not like I punch a clock for it. But if I had to really sit down and think about it, it's... it's a lot. I mean, it starts right when I wake up. Before I even get out of bed, I'm thinking about it. I'll lie there, and I can almost feel the roughness of my skin on the pillowcase, you know? And then I get up, and I have to go to the bathroom, and there's that mirror right there above the sink. I try not to look, I really do, but it's like a magnet. I'll catch a glimpse, and then I'm stuck there. I'll be leaning over the sink, getting right up close to the glass, looking at every single line, every deep groove around my mouth, the way my eyelids droop down... and the spots, God, the sun spots. I'll spend I don't know how long just staring, pulling at the skin, trying to see if maybe it doesn't look as bad from a different angle, but it always does. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm out working, which is where I'm most comfortable, it's still there in the back of my mind. I'll be on the tractor, and I'll look down at my hands on the steering wheel, and I'll see those thick, leathery knuckles, the dirt trapped in the cracks that I can't scrub out no matter how hard I try with the pumice stone. I'll just stare at my hands while I'm driving down the rows, thinking about how ugly they are, how they look like some sort of... animal claws or something. And if I have to stop and talk to the seed rep or the fertilizer guy when they come by the farm, well, then it's all I can think about. I'm trying to listen to what they're saying about nitrogen levels or whatever, but my brain is just screaming, \"They're looking at your face, Frank, they're looking at how your skin is sagging, they're disgusted.\" So, I mean, if you want an hour count... it's hours. Three, four, maybe more? It feels like it's just a constant hum in my brain all day long, from sunup to sundown, and even sometimes when I'm trying to fall asleep. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like working the land or being around family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's an interesting question, because I've been trying to figure that out myself. You see, the land, this farm... it's been my whole life. I took it over from my dad when I was just twenty-two, right after I did a couple years at the community college learning about soil science and agribusiness, thinking I was going to modernize the whole operation. And for forty years, I loved it. I loved the smell of the dirt turning over in the spring, I loved the quiet out there in the cab of the combine in the fall. It was my pride and joy. \n\nBut lately? I don't know. It's not that I don't care about the farm, it's just... I'm so distracted. Like I was saying about being on the tractor and just staring at my hands, it takes the joy right out of it. I used to look out over the fields and feel this great sense of accomplishment, but now I just look at my hands on the wheel and feel this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut. I'll catch sight of my reflection in the side mirror of the truck and it just ruins the whole morning. I don't even want to go to the diner for coffee with the other guys on Tuesday mornings anymore. We've been doing that for fifteen years, sitting at the back booth at Shirley's, talking about the weather and crop prices. But the last few times I went, I just sat there with my hands in my lap under the table, hoping nobody would look at my face in that harsh fluorescent light they have in there. I just felt miserable the whole time. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, my wife, like I said, she tries to be supportive, but she gets frustrated. Our daughter came up from the city last month with the grandkids. Usually, I'm right out there in the yard with them, pushing them on the tire swing, showing them the calves. But this time, I just... I stayed in the house a lot. I felt like if I went out there in the bright sunlight, they'd see every crack, every spot, every sagging piece of skin. I didn't want my grandkids looking at me and thinking their grandpa looked like some kind of ghoul. So I just sat in my recliner and watched them through the window. My wife was furious, asked me why I was being so sullen and withdrawn. I tried to explain it, but it just sounded crazy out loud. So, yeah, I guess I have been feeling pretty down. It's just hard to enjoy anything when you feel so... so ashamed of how you look all the time. It just colors everything, you know? Every single thing I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in most activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, \"hopeless\"... that's a mighty strong word, isn't it? I mean, when I hear \"hopeless,\" I think of a fella who just can't get out of bed at all, someone who just pulls the covers over his head and gives up on the whole world. And that's... that's not really me. Over these past couple of weeks, it's not like I've been walking around crying or feeling like life ain't worth living every single minute of the day. \n\nI still get up at 5:00 AM. I still make the coffee—Folgers, just like I've drank since 1982—and I sit there at the kitchen table and read the agricultural reports. I still care about the soybean prices, you know? I still want to know what the weather's doing. And just the other night, my wife and I were watching this history documentary on the television, the one about the Dust Bowl, and I was real interested in that. I was telling her all about the soil erosion techniques they should have been using back then, how they just tore up the deep-rooted grasses and let the wind take the topsoil. So, I haven't lost interest in *everything*, and I wouldn't say I'm down most of the day, every day. \n\nIt's really just... it's just what I was saying before. If I'm by myself in the machine shed, tinkering with the engine on the John Deere, I'm actually doing alright. I enjoy the work. I like figuring out why the belt is slipping or changing out the oil. It's only when the thought of my skin creeps in, or when I have to go be around folks and the light hits my face just right, or when I catch a look at my hands covered in those awful spots... that's when my mood just drops right out from under me. But as long as I can keep to myself and not think about how these deep cracks and sagging cheeks look to everybody else, I feel okay. I still love my life, I still love my farm. I just hate the way I look doing it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a constant hopelessness or losing interest in most things. It's just this one terrible thing that gets in the way of the things I do still care about.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about how your skin and hands look kept you from doing things you used to do, like going into town, meeting with other farmers, or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... yeah. It has. I mean, I touched on it a bit before with the diner and the grandkids, but it’s actually bleeding into the business side of things now, too, which is what really gets my goat. You see, running a farm these days, it’s not just driving a tractor. It’s a business. You’ve got to sit down with people. And lately... well, I’ve actually missed some pretty important calls with our bankers. We were supposed to be discussing the operating loan for next season, and I had the appointment all set up, but that morning, I just... I got stuck in the bathroom, trying to scrub my hands, trying to put some of my wife's lotion on my face to make the skin look less... dead, I guess. I just couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down across a desk from a professional in a suit, you know? So I just didn't go. I completely blew it off. I've put off some major farm decisions, just completely kicked the can down the road, all because I couldn't face sitting in a room with people who'd be staring at my leathery skin and these awful hands. \n\nAnd it's not just the farming side, either. My wife, she's been trying to diversify our income—a lot of folks are doing that now, agritourism they call it—so she set up this little weekend retreat on the property, and she does these craft sales, makes beautiful things. Well, in this day and age, you need a website, you need social media, and she's always wanting to take pictures of us to promote it. \"Frank, come stand by the new sign,\" or \"Frank, hold up this woodwork for the camera.\" And I used to do it, sure, smile for the camera. \n\nBut I've completely stopped agreeing to photos. I won't let her take a single picture of me. I know it would help promote the retreat, I know it helps her craft sales when people see the \"authentic farm family\" or whatever, but the thought of my face, my sagging cheeks and these huge, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone on the internet to zoom in on and scrutinize... I just can't do it. I absolutely refuse. It causes some real friction between us, because she doesn't understand why I won't just take two seconds to snap a picture, but I just... I can't let people see me like that, doc. It's just easier to stay out of sight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself doing things like checking mirrors often, touching your skin, or trying to cover up certain parts of your face or hands because of how they look?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that hits the nail right on the head, I suppose. I didn't really think about it as a... a habit, but yeah, I absolutely do that. It’s become a whole ordeal, to be honest with you. \n\nEspecially before I have to go somewhere where folks are going to be looking at me. Like I was saying about those loan meetings with the bank, or even if my wife actually manages to drag me out to sit behind one of her craft-fair tables for an hour to help her sell her woodwork. Before I even leave the house for something like that, I get stuck. I'll be in the bathroom, and the lighting in there is just brutal, but I can't pull myself away. I keep checking the bathroom mirror, leaning in close, looking at every deep line, every single sun spot. And then I'll grab my ballcap—usually my old green seed-company hat—and I'll put it on, pulling the brim down low. I'll stand there turning my hat this way and that, adjusting it a quarter inch to the left, then to the right, tilting it down, just trying to see if I can get it to cast a shadow over the worst of the sagging skin on my cheeks. I just keep fiddling with it, trying to cover up as much of my face as I can.\n\nAnd then, if I finally manage to make it out the back door, I get in the truck. And what do I do? I pull down the sun visor and start checking the truck mirror. I'll just sit there in the driveway with the engine idling, staring at my reflection all over again, making sure the hat is still covering what it needs to cover. \n\nAnd my hands... oh, Lord, my hands are a whole other story. When we have the seasonal boys come on, the younger workers who help out during planting or harvest, I catch myself just fixating on them. I'll be standing out by the barn, and I'll start comparing my hands to the younger workers' hands. I look at their skin—smooth, strong, no spots, no deep leathery cracks—and then I look down at my own spotted claws, and I just try to shove them deep into my jacket pockets so nobody else can compare them the way I am. \n\nAnd the worst part of it all, doc, the part that really makes me mad at myself, is the clock. I'll get caught in this whole loop of checking the mirrors and adjusting my hat and worrying about my hands, and suddenly I realize I've wasted forty-five minutes or an hour. I just keep doing it until I've wasted time I should be using on the farm. Time I should be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a fence, or actually getting to that bank meeting. It’s just... it's exhausting, is what it is. I just get so wrapped up in trying to hide how terrible it all looks that the whole morning just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to do certain things, like checking your appearance over and over or adjusting how you look, because it just felt like you had to, even if you didn’t want to?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's exactly what I was just getting at, really. It's like you're reading my mind, because that's the most frustrating part of the whole darn thing. It’s not like I *want* to be doing any of that. Lord knows I've got a hundred better things to do with my day. But it's exactly like you said—it feels like I absolutely *have* to. Like a tractor that's stuck in gear and you just can't pop the clutch out, no matter how hard you yank on it. \n\nLike I was just telling you a minute ago about getting ready for those loan meetings, or when my wife ropes me into sitting behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon... I don't walk into that bathroom thinking, \"Gee, I'd love to spend the next hour staring at my own face.\" I go in there because I feel this... this overwhelming pull, like I have to make sure it's not as horrifying as I think it is, or I have to find some way to hide it before I can face the world. I’ll stand there at the bathroom mirror, and then I’ll finally break away, but the second I get in the truck, I pull down that visor mirror, and I just feel compelled to keep checking. I’ll be turning that old green seed hat every which way, pulling it down, tilting it left, tilting it right, over and over again. I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Frank, for crying out loud, just put the truck in drive and go,\" but my hands just keep adjusting the brim. I literally can't stop myself until I feel like the shadows are hitting my sagging cheeks just right, even though I know in my gut it ain't really fixing the problem. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my hands. When I'm out there by the barn and I catch myself comparing my spotted, leathery hands to those younger workers we hire on for the season... I don't *want* to be doing that. It makes me feel miserable. It makes me feel like some old, decaying monster. But my eyes just dart right over to their hands—smooth, strong, not a single sun spot—and then back to my own claws, and I just have to keep comparing them. It's like my brain won't let it go. I'll shove my hands in my pockets, but my mind is still doing the comparing, still obsessing over it. \n\nI just keep doing it all—keep checking the mirrors, keep adjusting the hat, keep comparing myself to the young guys—until suddenly I look at my watch and I realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines or fixing a fence. But I just get completely trapped in it. It feels like a chore I never signed up for, but I can't clock out of it until my brain decides it's satisfied, if that makes any sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

This is a new candidate addition based on the current turn's content. No previous candidates (F22.0, F45.22) have been ruled out.",

I just can't do it. I absolutely refuse. It causes some real friction between us, because she doesn't understand why I won't just take two seconds to snap a picture, but I just... It’s become a whole ordeal, to be honest with you. \n\nEspecially before I have to go somewhere where folks are going to be looking at me. Before I even leave the house for something like that, I get stuck. I'll be in the bathroom, and the lighting in there is just brutal, but I can't pull myself away. I keep checking the bathroom mirror, leaning in close, looking at every deep line, every single sun spot. And then I'll grab my ballcap—usually my old green seed-company hat—and I'll put it on, pulling the brim down low. I'll stand there turning my hat this way and that, adjusting it a quarter inch to the left, then to the right, tilting it down, just trying to see if I can get it to cast a shadow over the worst of the sagging skin on my cheeks. I just keep fiddling with it, trying to cover up as much of my face as I can.\n\nAnd then, if I finally manage to make it out the back door, I get in the truck. And what do I do? I pull down the sun visor and start checking the truck mirror. I'll just sit there in the driveway with the engine idling, staring at my reflection all over again, making sure the hat is still covering what it needs to cover. \n\nAnd my hands... oh, Lord, my hands are a whole other story. When we have the seasonal boys come on, the younger workers who help out during planting or harvest, I catch myself just fixating on them. I'll be standing out by the barn, and I'll start comparing my hands to the younger workers' hands. I look at their skin—smooth, strong, no spots, no deep leathery cracks—and then I look down at my own spotted claws, and I just try to shove them deep into my jacket pockets so nobody else can compare them the way I am. \n\nAnd the worst part of it all, doc, the part that really makes me mad at myself, is the clock. I just keep doing it until I've wasted time I should be using on the farm. Time I should be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a fence, or actually getting to that bank meeting. It’s just... it's exhausting, is what it is. I literally can't stop myself until I feel like the shadows are hitting my sagging cheeks just right, even though I know in my gut it ain't really fixing the problem. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my hands. When I'm out there by the barn and I catch myself comparing my spotted, leathery hands to those younger workers we hire on for the season... I don't *want* to be doing that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, I feel like I might be beating a dead horse here since I’ve been bending your ear about it all morning, but yes. Absolutely. It’s... it’s really tearing up my life, to be completely honest with you. The distress is just constant. 

Like I was saying earlier about missing those calls with the bankers... that’s not just a minor scheduling hiccup, you know? That’s our livelihood on the line. When I put off those major farm decisions because I can’t get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down in a room with people, it creates a massive bottleneck. The seed reps are waiting on me, the equipment dealers need answers, and I’m just... I'm hiding. I completely blow off these critical meetings because the panic of having some professional in a suit staring at my dead, leathery skin is just too much to bear. It makes me feel like a complete failure of a provider, but I still can't force myself to go. 

And it’s the same with my marriage and the business my wife is trying to build. I mentioned she’s doing the agritourism thing, the weekend retreat and her craft sales. The fact that I've completely stopped agreeing to photos... it's not just a polite 'no thank you' when she asks. It causes real, heavy friction between us. She works so incredibly hard on her woodwork, and she just wants a simple picture of us by the new sign to put on the internet to promote the retreat. She knows people want to see the authentic farm family—it helps sales! I know it helps sales! But the sheer terror I feel at the thought of my sagging cheeks and these... these awful, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone to zoom in on... it makes my chest physically ache. 

I know I'm letting her down. I know I'm hurting the promotion of the retreat. But I absolutely refuse to do it. So, yeah, it interferes with my work, it puts a wedge in my marriage, it ruins my mornings... it just feels like this heavy wet blanket over every single part of my daily life, doc.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F45.22, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain without sounding foolish, I suppose. I mean, I'm 62 years old, I've worked the land my whole life, been out in the sun, the wind, the dirt. You expect some wear and tear, right? But lately, I just... I can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. It's not just a passing thought, it's like this heavy thing sitting on my chest all day. I look in the mirror and all I see are these deep, jagged cracks in my skin, these dark sun spots that seem to be multiplying, and the way my cheeks are just sagging down. It's like my face is melting off my skull. \n\nAnd it's not just when I'm at home. When I have to go into town, say, to the bank to talk about the farm loan, or to the county office for some paperwork... I can feel them staring. I know they're looking right at my hands when I sign a paper, looking at the dirt that never seems to wash out of the creases, looking at how leathery and awful I look. I feel like they're judging me, thinking I'm some sort of... I don't know, some sort of monster. My wife, she tells me I'm crazy. She says, \"Frank, you just look like a man who's worked outdoors his whole life, there's nothing wrong with you.\" But she doesn't see what I see. She doesn't feel the eyes on her when she walks into a room. It's gotten to the point where I don't even want to go into town anymore, I just want to stay out in the fields where nobody can see me. It's exhausting, doc, it really is. I just can't stop thinking about how terrible I look.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend thinking about your appearance on a typical day?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a tough one to put a number on, you know? It's not like I punch a clock for it. But if I had to really sit down and think about it, it's... it's a lot. I mean, it starts right when I wake up. Before I even get out of bed, I'm thinking about it. I'll lie there, and I can almost feel the roughness of my skin on the pillowcase, you know? And then I get up, and I have to go to the bathroom, and there's that mirror right there above the sink. I try not to look, I really do, but it's like a magnet. I'll catch a glimpse, and then I'm stuck there. I'll be leaning over the sink, getting right up close to the glass, looking at every single line, every deep groove around my mouth, the way my eyelids droop down... and the spots, God, the sun spots. I'll spend I don't know how long just staring, pulling at the skin, trying to see if maybe it doesn't look as bad from a different angle, but it always does. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm out working, which is where I'm most comfortable, it's still there in the back of my mind. I'll be on the tractor, and I'll look down at my hands on the steering wheel, and I'll see those thick, leathery knuckles, the dirt trapped in the cracks that I can't scrub out no matter how hard I try with the pumice stone. I'll just stare at my hands while I'm driving down the rows, thinking about how ugly they are, how they look like some sort of... animal claws or something. And if I have to stop and talk to the seed rep or the fertilizer guy when they come by the farm, well, then it's all I can think about. I'm trying to listen to what they're saying about nitrogen levels or whatever, but my brain is just screaming, \"They're looking at your face, Frank, they're looking at how your skin is sagging, they're disgusted.\" So, I mean, if you want an hour count... it's hours. Three, four, maybe more? It feels like it's just a constant hum in my brain all day long, from sunup to sundown, and even sometimes when I'm trying to fall asleep. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like working the land or being around family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's an interesting question, because I've been trying to figure that out myself. You see, the land, this farm... it's been my whole life. I took it over from my dad when I was just twenty-two, right after I did a couple years at the community college learning about soil science and agribusiness, thinking I was going to modernize the whole operation. And for forty years, I loved it. I loved the smell of the dirt turning over in the spring, I loved the quiet out there in the cab of the combine in the fall. It was my pride and joy. \n\nBut lately? I don't know. It's not that I don't care about the farm, it's just... I'm so distracted. Like I was saying about being on the tractor and just staring at my hands, it takes the joy right out of it. I used to look out over the fields and feel this great sense of accomplishment, but now I just look at my hands on the wheel and feel this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut. I'll catch sight of my reflection in the side mirror of the truck and it just ruins the whole morning. I don't even want to go to the diner for coffee with the other guys on Tuesday mornings anymore. We've been doing that for fifteen years, sitting at the back booth at Shirley's, talking about the weather and crop prices. But the last few times I went, I just sat there with my hands in my lap under the table, hoping nobody would look at my face in that harsh fluorescent light they have in there. I just felt miserable the whole time. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, my wife, like I said, she tries to be supportive, but she gets frustrated. Our daughter came up from the city last month with the grandkids. Usually, I'm right out there in the yard with them, pushing them on the tire swing, showing them the calves. But this time, I just... I stayed in the house a lot. I felt like if I went out there in the bright sunlight, they'd see every crack, every spot, every sagging piece of skin. I didn't want my grandkids looking at me and thinking their grandpa looked like some kind of ghoul. So I just sat in my recliner and watched them through the window. My wife was furious, asked me why I was being so sullen and withdrawn. I tried to explain it, but it just sounded crazy out loud. So, yeah, I guess I have been feeling pretty down. It's just hard to enjoy anything when you feel so... so ashamed of how you look all the time. It just colors everything, you know? Every single thing I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in most activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, \"hopeless\"... that's a mighty strong word, isn't it? I mean, when I hear \"hopeless,\" I think of a fella who just can't get out of bed at all, someone who just pulls the covers over his head and gives up on the whole world. And that's... that's not really me. Over these past couple of weeks, it's not like I've been walking around crying or feeling like life ain't worth living every single minute of the day. \n\nI still get up at 5:00 AM. I still make the coffee—Folgers, just like I've drank since 1982—and I sit there at the kitchen table and read the agricultural reports. I still care about the soybean prices, you know? I still want to know what the weather's doing. And just the other night, my wife and I were watching this history documentary on the television, the one about the Dust Bowl, and I was real interested in that. I was telling her all about the soil erosion techniques they should have been using back then, how they just tore up the deep-rooted grasses and let the wind take the topsoil. So, I haven't lost interest in *everything*, and I wouldn't say I'm down most of the day, every day. \n\nIt's really just... it's just what I was saying before. If I'm by myself in the machine shed, tinkering with the engine on the John Deere, I'm actually doing alright. I enjoy the work. I like figuring out why the belt is slipping or changing out the oil. It's only when the thought of my skin creeps in, or when I have to go be around folks and the light hits my face just right, or when I catch a look at my hands covered in those awful spots... that's when my mood just drops right out from under me. But as long as I can keep to myself and not think about how these deep cracks and sagging cheeks look to everybody else, I feel okay. I still love my life, I still love my farm. I just hate the way I look doing it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a constant hopelessness or losing interest in most things. It's just this one terrible thing that gets in the way of the things I do still care about.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about how your skin and hands look kept you from doing things you used to do, like going into town, meeting with other farmers, or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... yeah. It has. I mean, I touched on it a bit before with the diner and the grandkids, but it’s actually bleeding into the business side of things now, too, which is what really gets my goat. You see, running a farm these days, it’s not just driving a tractor. It’s a business. You’ve got to sit down with people. And lately... well, I’ve actually missed some pretty important calls with our bankers. We were supposed to be discussing the operating loan for next season, and I had the appointment all set up, but that morning, I just... I got stuck in the bathroom, trying to scrub my hands, trying to put some of my wife's lotion on my face to make the skin look less... dead, I guess. I just couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down across a desk from a professional in a suit, you know? So I just didn't go. I completely blew it off. I've put off some major farm decisions, just completely kicked the can down the road, all because I couldn't face sitting in a room with people who'd be staring at my leathery skin and these awful hands. \n\nAnd it's not just the farming side, either. My wife, she's been trying to diversify our income—a lot of folks are doing that now, agritourism they call it—so she set up this little weekend retreat on the property, and she does these craft sales, makes beautiful things. Well, in this day and age, you need a website, you need social media, and she's always wanting to take pictures of us to promote it. \"Frank, come stand by the new sign,\" or \"Frank, hold up this woodwork for the camera.\" And I used to do it, sure, smile for the camera. \n\nBut I've completely stopped agreeing to photos. I won't let her take a single picture of me. I know it would help promote the retreat, I know it helps her craft sales when people see the \"authentic farm family\" or whatever, but the thought of my face, my sagging cheeks and these huge, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone on the internet to zoom in on and scrutinize... I just can't do it. I absolutely refuse. It causes some real friction between us, because she doesn't understand why I won't just take two seconds to snap a picture, but I just... I can't let people see me like that, doc. It's just easier to stay out of sight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself doing things like checking mirrors often, touching your skin, or trying to cover up certain parts of your face or hands because of how they look?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that hits the nail right on the head, I suppose. I didn't really think about it as a... a habit, but yeah, I absolutely do that. It’s become a whole ordeal, to be honest with you. \n\nEspecially before I have to go somewhere where folks are going to be looking at me. Like I was saying about those loan meetings with the bank, or even if my wife actually manages to drag me out to sit behind one of her craft-fair tables for an hour to help her sell her woodwork. Before I even leave the house for something like that, I get stuck. I'll be in the bathroom, and the lighting in there is just brutal, but I can't pull myself away. I keep checking the bathroom mirror, leaning in close, looking at every deep line, every single sun spot. And then I'll grab my ballcap—usually my old green seed-company hat—and I'll put it on, pulling the brim down low. I'll stand there turning my hat this way and that, adjusting it a quarter inch to the left, then to the right, tilting it down, just trying to see if I can get it to cast a shadow over the worst of the sagging skin on my cheeks. I just keep fiddling with it, trying to cover up as much of my face as I can.\n\nAnd then, if I finally manage to make it out the back door, I get in the truck. And what do I do? I pull down the sun visor and start checking the truck mirror. I'll just sit there in the driveway with the engine idling, staring at my reflection all over again, making sure the hat is still covering what it needs to cover. \n\nAnd my hands... oh, Lord, my hands are a whole other story. When we have the seasonal boys come on, the younger workers who help out during planting or harvest, I catch myself just fixating on them. I'll be standing out by the barn, and I'll start comparing my hands to the younger workers' hands. I look at their skin—smooth, strong, no spots, no deep leathery cracks—and then I look down at my own spotted claws, and I just try to shove them deep into my jacket pockets so nobody else can compare them the way I am. \n\nAnd the worst part of it all, doc, the part that really makes me mad at myself, is the clock. I'll get caught in this whole loop of checking the mirrors and adjusting my hat and worrying about my hands, and suddenly I realize I've wasted forty-five minutes or an hour. I just keep doing it until I've wasted time I should be using on the farm. Time I should be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a fence, or actually getting to that bank meeting. It’s just... it's exhausting, is what it is. I just get so wrapped up in trying to hide how terrible it all looks that the whole morning just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to do certain things, like checking your appearance over and over or adjusting how you look, because it just felt like you had to, even if you didn’t want to?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's exactly what I was just getting at, really. It's like you're reading my mind, because that's the most frustrating part of the whole darn thing. It’s not like I *want* to be doing any of that. Lord knows I've got a hundred better things to do with my day. But it's exactly like you said—it feels like I absolutely *have* to. Like a tractor that's stuck in gear and you just can't pop the clutch out, no matter how hard you yank on it. \n\nLike I was just telling you a minute ago about getting ready for those loan meetings, or when my wife ropes me into sitting behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon... I don't walk into that bathroom thinking, \"Gee, I'd love to spend the next hour staring at my own face.\" I go in there because I feel this... this overwhelming pull, like I have to make sure it's not as horrifying as I think it is, or I have to find some way to hide it before I can face the world. I’ll stand there at the bathroom mirror, and then I’ll finally break away, but the second I get in the truck, I pull down that visor mirror, and I just feel compelled to keep checking. I’ll be turning that old green seed hat every which way, pulling it down, tilting it left, tilting it right, over and over again. I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Frank, for crying out loud, just put the truck in drive and go,\" but my hands just keep adjusting the brim. I literally can't stop myself until I feel like the shadows are hitting my sagging cheeks just right, even though I know in my gut it ain't really fixing the problem. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my hands. When I'm out there by the barn and I catch myself comparing my spotted, leathery hands to those younger workers we hire on for the season... I don't *want* to be doing that. It makes me feel miserable. It makes me feel like some old, decaying monster. But my eyes just dart right over to their hands—smooth, strong, not a single sun spot—and then back to my own claws, and I just have to keep comparing them. It's like my brain won't let it go. I'll shove my hands in my pockets, but my mind is still doing the comparing, still obsessing over it. \n\nI just keep doing it all—keep checking the mirrors, keep adjusting the hat, keep comparing myself to the young guys—until suddenly I look at my watch and I realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines or fixing a fence. But I just get completely trapped in it. It feels like a chore I never signed up for, but I can't clock out of it until my brain decides it's satisfied, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about how your skin and hands look ever caused you significant distress or interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like I might be beating a dead horse here since I’ve been bending your ear about it all morning, but yes. Absolutely. It’s... it’s really tearing up my life, to be completely honest with you. The distress is just constant. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about missing those calls with the bankers... that’s not just a minor scheduling hiccup, you know? That’s our livelihood on the line. When I put off those major farm decisions because I can’t get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down in a room with people, it creates a massive bottleneck. The seed reps are waiting on me, the equipment dealers need answers, and I’m just... I'm hiding. I completely blow off these critical meetings because the panic of having some professional in a suit staring at my dead, leathery skin is just too much to bear. It makes me feel like a complete failure of a provider, but I still can't force myself to go. \n\nAnd it’s the same with my marriage and the business my wife is trying to build. I mentioned she’s doing the agritourism thing, the weekend retreat and her craft sales. The fact that I've completely stopped agreeing to photos... it's not just a polite 'no thank you' when she asks. It causes real, heavy friction between us. She works so incredibly hard on her woodwork, and she just wants a simple picture of us by the new sign to put on the internet to promote the retreat. She knows people want to see the authentic farm family—it helps sales! I know it helps sales! But the sheer terror I feel at the thought of my sagging cheeks and these... these awful, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone to zoom in on... it makes my chest physically ache. \n\nI know I'm letting her down. I know I'm hurting the promotion of the retreat. But I absolutely refuse to do it. So, yeah, it interferes with my work, it puts a wedge in my marriage, it ruins my mornings... it just feels like this heavy wet blanket over every single part of my daily life, doc.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, I gotta ask, are you testing my memory here or did we just take a wrong turn in the conversation? Because I feel like I'm beating the exact same drum I've been beating for the last twenty minutes! But yes, absolutely, yes. I do exactly that, and it's just like I was telling you before. 

It’s the same old song and dance every single time. Before I even try to leave the house for one of those loan meetings with the bank, or before my wife makes me go sit behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon, I get completely stuck in this... this loop. I'll be staring into the bathroom mirror, just fixated, and then if I finally manage to break away and get outside, I'm right back at it in the truck mirror. I'll sit there with the engine running, pulling the visor down, turning that old green hat of mine this way and that, adjusting the brim a fraction of an inch at a time, just praying it hides the worst of my sagging cheeks. 

And the mental part of it, like you asked about, thinking about how others see me... that's exactly what happens when I'm looking at my hands. I'll be out in the yard, and I just start comparing my hands to the younger workers we hire on for the season. I'll be mentally going over how smooth and strong their skin is, and then looking at my own spotted claws, just obsessing over the difference. I know it doesn't make a lick of sense. I'm a sixty-two-year-old man, I know that turning my hat for the fifteenth time or staring at a twenty-year-old's hands isn't going to fix my skin. 

But I just feel like I have to keep doing it. I keep checking the mirrors, keep fiddling with the hat, keep comparing myself to those young guys, until suddenly I look at the clock and realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a busted fence post, or actually getting to the bank. I've run a farm for decades, I know how to manage a crew and manage my day, but when it comes to this... I just can't seem to walk away from a mirror without losing half my morning to it. It’s baffling, it really is.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain without sounding foolish, I suppose. I mean, I'm 62 years old, I've worked the land my whole life, been out in the sun, the wind, the dirt. You expect some wear and tear, right? But lately, I just... I can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. It's not just a passing thought, it's like this heavy thing sitting on my chest all day. I look in the mirror and all I see are these deep, jagged cracks in my skin, these dark sun spots that seem to be multiplying, and the way my cheeks are just sagging down. It's like my face is melting off my skull. \n\nAnd it's not just when I'm at home. When I have to go into town, say, to the bank to talk about the farm loan, or to the county office for some paperwork... I can feel them staring. I know they're looking right at my hands when I sign a paper, looking at the dirt that never seems to wash out of the creases, looking at how leathery and awful I look. I feel like they're judging me, thinking I'm some sort of... I don't know, some sort of monster. My wife, she tells me I'm crazy. She says, \"Frank, you just look like a man who's worked outdoors his whole life, there's nothing wrong with you.\" But she doesn't see what I see. She doesn't feel the eyes on her when she walks into a room. It's gotten to the point where I don't even want to go into town anymore, I just want to stay out in the fields where nobody can see me. It's exhausting, doc, it really is. I just can't stop thinking about how terrible I look.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend thinking about your appearance on a typical day?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a tough one to put a number on, you know? It's not like I punch a clock for it. But if I had to really sit down and think about it, it's... it's a lot. I mean, it starts right when I wake up. Before I even get out of bed, I'm thinking about it. I'll lie there, and I can almost feel the roughness of my skin on the pillowcase, you know? And then I get up, and I have to go to the bathroom, and there's that mirror right there above the sink. I try not to look, I really do, but it's like a magnet. I'll catch a glimpse, and then I'm stuck there. I'll be leaning over the sink, getting right up close to the glass, looking at every single line, every deep groove around my mouth, the way my eyelids droop down... and the spots, God, the sun spots. I'll spend I don't know how long just staring, pulling at the skin, trying to see if maybe it doesn't look as bad from a different angle, but it always does. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm out working, which is where I'm most comfortable, it's still there in the back of my mind. I'll be on the tractor, and I'll look down at my hands on the steering wheel, and I'll see those thick, leathery knuckles, the dirt trapped in the cracks that I can't scrub out no matter how hard I try with the pumice stone. I'll just stare at my hands while I'm driving down the rows, thinking about how ugly they are, how they look like some sort of... animal claws or something. And if I have to stop and talk to the seed rep or the fertilizer guy when they come by the farm, well, then it's all I can think about. I'm trying to listen to what they're saying about nitrogen levels or whatever, but my brain is just screaming, \"They're looking at your face, Frank, they're looking at how your skin is sagging, they're disgusted.\" So, I mean, if you want an hour count... it's hours. Three, four, maybe more? It feels like it's just a constant hum in my brain all day long, from sunup to sundown, and even sometimes when I'm trying to fall asleep. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like working the land or being around family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's an interesting question, because I've been trying to figure that out myself. You see, the land, this farm... it's been my whole life. I took it over from my dad when I was just twenty-two, right after I did a couple years at the community college learning about soil science and agribusiness, thinking I was going to modernize the whole operation. And for forty years, I loved it. I loved the smell of the dirt turning over in the spring, I loved the quiet out there in the cab of the combine in the fall. It was my pride and joy. \n\nBut lately? I don't know. It's not that I don't care about the farm, it's just... I'm so distracted. Like I was saying about being on the tractor and just staring at my hands, it takes the joy right out of it. I used to look out over the fields and feel this great sense of accomplishment, but now I just look at my hands on the wheel and feel this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut. I'll catch sight of my reflection in the side mirror of the truck and it just ruins the whole morning. I don't even want to go to the diner for coffee with the other guys on Tuesday mornings anymore. We've been doing that for fifteen years, sitting at the back booth at Shirley's, talking about the weather and crop prices. But the last few times I went, I just sat there with my hands in my lap under the table, hoping nobody would look at my face in that harsh fluorescent light they have in there. I just felt miserable the whole time. \n\nAnd as for my family... well, my wife, like I said, she tries to be supportive, but she gets frustrated. Our daughter came up from the city last month with the grandkids. Usually, I'm right out there in the yard with them, pushing them on the tire swing, showing them the calves. But this time, I just... I stayed in the house a lot. I felt like if I went out there in the bright sunlight, they'd see every crack, every spot, every sagging piece of skin. I didn't want my grandkids looking at me and thinking their grandpa looked like some kind of ghoul. So I just sat in my recliner and watched them through the window. My wife was furious, asked me why I was being so sullen and withdrawn. I tried to explain it, but it just sounded crazy out loud. So, yeah, I guess I have been feeling pretty down. It's just hard to enjoy anything when you feel so... so ashamed of how you look all the time. It just colors everything, you know? Every single thing I do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in most activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, \"hopeless\"... that's a mighty strong word, isn't it? I mean, when I hear \"hopeless,\" I think of a fella who just can't get out of bed at all, someone who just pulls the covers over his head and gives up on the whole world. And that's... that's not really me. Over these past couple of weeks, it's not like I've been walking around crying or feeling like life ain't worth living every single minute of the day. \n\nI still get up at 5:00 AM. I still make the coffee—Folgers, just like I've drank since 1982—and I sit there at the kitchen table and read the agricultural reports. I still care about the soybean prices, you know? I still want to know what the weather's doing. And just the other night, my wife and I were watching this history documentary on the television, the one about the Dust Bowl, and I was real interested in that. I was telling her all about the soil erosion techniques they should have been using back then, how they just tore up the deep-rooted grasses and let the wind take the topsoil. So, I haven't lost interest in *everything*, and I wouldn't say I'm down most of the day, every day. \n\nIt's really just... it's just what I was saying before. If I'm by myself in the machine shed, tinkering with the engine on the John Deere, I'm actually doing alright. I enjoy the work. I like figuring out why the belt is slipping or changing out the oil. It's only when the thought of my skin creeps in, or when I have to go be around folks and the light hits my face just right, or when I catch a look at my hands covered in those awful spots... that's when my mood just drops right out from under me. But as long as I can keep to myself and not think about how these deep cracks and sagging cheeks look to everybody else, I feel okay. I still love my life, I still love my farm. I just hate the way I look doing it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a constant hopelessness or losing interest in most things. It's just this one terrible thing that gets in the way of the things I do still care about.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about how your skin and hands look kept you from doing things you used to do, like going into town, meeting with other farmers, or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... yeah. It has. I mean, I touched on it a bit before with the diner and the grandkids, but it’s actually bleeding into the business side of things now, too, which is what really gets my goat. You see, running a farm these days, it’s not just driving a tractor. It’s a business. You’ve got to sit down with people. And lately... well, I’ve actually missed some pretty important calls with our bankers. We were supposed to be discussing the operating loan for next season, and I had the appointment all set up, but that morning, I just... I got stuck in the bathroom, trying to scrub my hands, trying to put some of my wife's lotion on my face to make the skin look less... dead, I guess. I just couldn't get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down across a desk from a professional in a suit, you know? So I just didn't go. I completely blew it off. I've put off some major farm decisions, just completely kicked the can down the road, all because I couldn't face sitting in a room with people who'd be staring at my leathery skin and these awful hands. \n\nAnd it's not just the farming side, either. My wife, she's been trying to diversify our income—a lot of folks are doing that now, agritourism they call it—so she set up this little weekend retreat on the property, and she does these craft sales, makes beautiful things. Well, in this day and age, you need a website, you need social media, and she's always wanting to take pictures of us to promote it. \"Frank, come stand by the new sign,\" or \"Frank, hold up this woodwork for the camera.\" And I used to do it, sure, smile for the camera. \n\nBut I've completely stopped agreeing to photos. I won't let her take a single picture of me. I know it would help promote the retreat, I know it helps her craft sales when people see the \"authentic farm family\" or whatever, but the thought of my face, my sagging cheeks and these huge, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone on the internet to zoom in on and scrutinize... I just can't do it. I absolutely refuse. It causes some real friction between us, because she doesn't understand why I won't just take two seconds to snap a picture, but I just... I can't let people see me like that, doc. It's just easier to stay out of sight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself doing things like checking mirrors often, touching your skin, or trying to cover up certain parts of your face or hands because of how they look?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that hits the nail right on the head, I suppose. I didn't really think about it as a... a habit, but yeah, I absolutely do that. It’s become a whole ordeal, to be honest with you. \n\nEspecially before I have to go somewhere where folks are going to be looking at me. Like I was saying about those loan meetings with the bank, or even if my wife actually manages to drag me out to sit behind one of her craft-fair tables for an hour to help her sell her woodwork. Before I even leave the house for something like that, I get stuck. I'll be in the bathroom, and the lighting in there is just brutal, but I can't pull myself away. I keep checking the bathroom mirror, leaning in close, looking at every deep line, every single sun spot. And then I'll grab my ballcap—usually my old green seed-company hat—and I'll put it on, pulling the brim down low. I'll stand there turning my hat this way and that, adjusting it a quarter inch to the left, then to the right, tilting it down, just trying to see if I can get it to cast a shadow over the worst of the sagging skin on my cheeks. I just keep fiddling with it, trying to cover up as much of my face as I can.\n\nAnd then, if I finally manage to make it out the back door, I get in the truck. And what do I do? I pull down the sun visor and start checking the truck mirror. I'll just sit there in the driveway with the engine idling, staring at my reflection all over again, making sure the hat is still covering what it needs to cover. \n\nAnd my hands... oh, Lord, my hands are a whole other story. When we have the seasonal boys come on, the younger workers who help out during planting or harvest, I catch myself just fixating on them. I'll be standing out by the barn, and I'll start comparing my hands to the younger workers' hands. I look at their skin—smooth, strong, no spots, no deep leathery cracks—and then I look down at my own spotted claws, and I just try to shove them deep into my jacket pockets so nobody else can compare them the way I am. \n\nAnd the worst part of it all, doc, the part that really makes me mad at myself, is the clock. I'll get caught in this whole loop of checking the mirrors and adjusting my hat and worrying about my hands, and suddenly I realize I've wasted forty-five minutes or an hour. I just keep doing it until I've wasted time I should be using on the farm. Time I should be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a fence, or actually getting to that bank meeting. It’s just... it's exhausting, is what it is. I just get so wrapped up in trying to hide how terrible it all looks that the whole morning just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to do certain things, like checking your appearance over and over or adjusting how you look, because it just felt like you had to, even if you didn’t want to?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's exactly what I was just getting at, really. It's like you're reading my mind, because that's the most frustrating part of the whole darn thing. It’s not like I *want* to be doing any of that. Lord knows I've got a hundred better things to do with my day. But it's exactly like you said—it feels like I absolutely *have* to. Like a tractor that's stuck in gear and you just can't pop the clutch out, no matter how hard you yank on it. \n\nLike I was just telling you a minute ago about getting ready for those loan meetings, or when my wife ropes me into sitting behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon... I don't walk into that bathroom thinking, \"Gee, I'd love to spend the next hour staring at my own face.\" I go in there because I feel this... this overwhelming pull, like I have to make sure it's not as horrifying as I think it is, or I have to find some way to hide it before I can face the world. I’ll stand there at the bathroom mirror, and then I’ll finally break away, but the second I get in the truck, I pull down that visor mirror, and I just feel compelled to keep checking. I’ll be turning that old green seed hat every which way, pulling it down, tilting it left, tilting it right, over and over again. I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Frank, for crying out loud, just put the truck in drive and go,\" but my hands just keep adjusting the brim. I literally can't stop myself until I feel like the shadows are hitting my sagging cheeks just right, even though I know in my gut it ain't really fixing the problem. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my hands. When I'm out there by the barn and I catch myself comparing my spotted, leathery hands to those younger workers we hire on for the season... I don't *want* to be doing that. It makes me feel miserable. It makes me feel like some old, decaying monster. But my eyes just dart right over to their hands—smooth, strong, not a single sun spot—and then back to my own claws, and I just have to keep comparing them. It's like my brain won't let it go. I'll shove my hands in my pockets, but my mind is still doing the comparing, still obsessing over it. \n\nI just keep doing it all—keep checking the mirrors, keep adjusting the hat, keep comparing myself to the young guys—until suddenly I look at my watch and I realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines or fixing a fence. But I just get completely trapped in it. It feels like a chore I never signed up for, but I can't clock out of it until my brain decides it's satisfied, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about how your skin and hands look ever caused you significant distress or interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like I might be beating a dead horse here since I’ve been bending your ear about it all morning, but yes. Absolutely. It’s... it’s really tearing up my life, to be completely honest with you. The distress is just constant. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about missing those calls with the bankers... that’s not just a minor scheduling hiccup, you know? That’s our livelihood on the line. When I put off those major farm decisions because I can’t get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down in a room with people, it creates a massive bottleneck. The seed reps are waiting on me, the equipment dealers need answers, and I’m just... I'm hiding. I completely blow off these critical meetings because the panic of having some professional in a suit staring at my dead, leathery skin is just too much to bear. It makes me feel like a complete failure of a provider, but I still can't force myself to go. \n\nAnd it’s the same with my marriage and the business my wife is trying to build. I mentioned she’s doing the agritourism thing, the weekend retreat and her craft sales. The fact that I've completely stopped agreeing to photos... it's not just a polite 'no thank you' when she asks. It causes real, heavy friction between us. She works so incredibly hard on her woodwork, and she just wants a simple picture of us by the new sign to put on the internet to promote the retreat. She knows people want to see the authentic farm family—it helps sales! I know it helps sales! But the sheer terror I feel at the thought of my sagging cheeks and these... these awful, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone to zoom in on... it makes my chest physically ache. \n\nI know I'm letting her down. I know I'm hurting the promotion of the retreat. But I absolutely refuse to do it. So, yeah, it interferes with my work, it puts a wedge in my marriage, it ruins my mornings... it just feels like this heavy wet blanket over every single part of my daily life, doc.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself repeating certain behaviors, like checking your appearance, adjusting how you look, or mentally going over how you think others see you, because it feels like you have to, even when you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I gotta ask, are you testing my memory here or did we just take a wrong turn in the conversation? Because I feel like I'm beating the exact same drum I've been beating for the last twenty minutes! But yes, absolutely, yes. I do exactly that, and it's just like I was telling you before. \n\nIt’s the same old song and dance every single time. Before I even try to leave the house for one of those loan meetings with the bank, or before my wife makes me go sit behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon, I get completely stuck in this... this loop. I'll be staring into the bathroom mirror, just fixated, and then if I finally manage to break away and get outside, I'm right back at it in the truck mirror. I'll sit there with the engine running, pulling the visor down, turning that old green hat of mine this way and that, adjusting the brim a fraction of an inch at a time, just praying it hides the worst of my sagging cheeks. \n\nAnd the mental part of it, like you asked about, thinking about how others see me... that's exactly what happens when I'm looking at my hands. I'll be out in the yard, and I just start comparing my hands to the younger workers we hire on for the season. I'll be mentally going over how smooth and strong their skin is, and then looking at my own spotted claws, just obsessing over the difference. I know it doesn't make a lick of sense. I'm a sixty-two-year-old man, I know that turning my hat for the fifteenth time or staring at a twenty-year-old's hands isn't going to fix my skin. \n\nBut I just feel like I have to keep doing it. I keep checking the mirrors, keep fiddling with the hat, keep comparing myself to those young guys, until suddenly I look at the clock and realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a busted fence post, or actually getting to the bank. I've run a farm for decades, I know how to manage a crew and manage my day, but when it comes to this... I just can't seem to walk away from a mirror without losing half my morning to it. It’s baffling, it really is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I look at their skin—smooth, strong, no spots, no deep leathery cracks—and then I look down at my own spotted claws, and I just try to shove them deep into my jacket pockets so nobody else can compare them the way I am. \n\nAnd the worst part of it all, doc, the part that really makes me mad at myself, is the clock. I'll get caught in this whole loop of checking the mirrors and adjusting my hat and worrying about my hands, and suddenly I realize I've wasted forty-five minutes or an hour. I just keep doing it until I've wasted time I should be using on the farm. Time I should be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a fence, or actually getting to that bank meeting. It’s just... it's exhausting, is what it is. I just get so wrapped up in trying to hide how terrible it all looks that the whole morning just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to do certain things, like checking your appearance over and over or adjusting how you look, because it just felt like you had to, even if you didn’t want to?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's exactly what I was just getting at, really. It's like you're reading my mind, because that's the most frustrating part of the whole darn thing. It’s not like I *want* to be doing any of that. Lord knows I've got a hundred better things to do with my day. But it's exactly like you said—it feels like I absolutely *have* to. Like a tractor that's stuck in gear and you just can't pop the clutch out, no matter how hard you yank on it. \n\nLike I was just telling you a minute ago about getting ready for those loan meetings, or when my wife ropes me into sitting behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon... I don't walk into that bathroom thinking, \"Gee, I'd love to spend the next hour staring at my own face.\" I go in there because I feel this... this overwhelming pull, like I have to make sure it's not as horrifying as I think it is, or I have to find some way to hide it before I can face the world. I’ll stand there at the bathroom mirror, and then I’ll finally break away, but the second I get in the truck, I pull down that visor mirror, and I just feel compelled to keep checking. I’ll be turning that old green seed hat every which way, pulling it down, tilting it left, tilting it right, over and over again. I'll be sitting there telling myself, \"Frank, for crying out loud, just put the truck in drive and go,\" but my hands just keep adjusting the brim. I literally can't stop myself until I feel like the shadows are hitting my sagging cheeks just right, even though I know in my gut it ain't really fixing the problem. \n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my hands. When I'm out there by the barn and I catch myself comparing my spotted, leathery hands to those younger workers we hire on for the season... I don't *want* to be doing that. It makes me feel miserable. It makes me feel like some old, decaying monster. But my eyes just dart right over to their hands—smooth, strong, not a single sun spot—and then back to my own claws, and I just have to keep comparing them. It's like my brain won't let it go. I'll shove my hands in my pockets, but my mind is still doing the comparing, still obsessing over it. \n\nI just keep doing it all—keep checking the mirrors, keep adjusting the hat, keep comparing myself to the young guys—until suddenly I look at my watch and I realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines or fixing a fence. But I just get completely trapped in it. It feels like a chore I never signed up for, but I can't clock out of it until my brain decides it's satisfied, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about how your skin and hands look ever caused you significant distress or interfered with your work, relationships, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like I might be beating a dead horse here since I’ve been bending your ear about it all morning, but yes. Absolutely. It’s... it’s really tearing up my life, to be completely honest with you. The distress is just constant. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about missing those calls with the bankers... that’s not just a minor scheduling hiccup, you know? That’s our livelihood on the line. When I put off those major farm decisions because I can’t get myself looking acceptable enough to sit down in a room with people, it creates a massive bottleneck. The seed reps are waiting on me, the equipment dealers need answers, and I’m just... I'm hiding. I completely blow off these critical meetings because the panic of having some professional in a suit staring at my dead, leathery skin is just too much to bear. It makes me feel like a complete failure of a provider, but I still can't force myself to go. \n\nAnd it’s the same with my marriage and the business my wife is trying to build. I mentioned she’s doing the agritourism thing, the weekend retreat and her craft sales. The fact that I've completely stopped agreeing to photos... it's not just a polite 'no thank you' when she asks. It causes real, heavy friction between us. She works so incredibly hard on her woodwork, and she just wants a simple picture of us by the new sign to put on the internet to promote the retreat. She knows people want to see the authentic farm family—it helps sales! I know it helps sales! But the sheer terror I feel at the thought of my sagging cheeks and these... these awful, spotted claws of mine being frozen in a picture for everyone to zoom in on... it makes my chest physically ache. \n\nI know I'm letting her down. I know I'm hurting the promotion of the retreat. But I absolutely refuse to do it. So, yeah, it interferes with my work, it puts a wedge in my marriage, it ruins my mornings... it just feels like this heavy wet blanket over every single part of my daily life, doc.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself repeating certain behaviors, like checking your appearance, adjusting how you look, or mentally going over how you think others see you, because it feels like you have to, even when you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I gotta ask, are you testing my memory here or did we just take a wrong turn in the conversation? Because I feel like I'm beating the exact same drum I've been beating for the last twenty minutes! But yes, absolutely, yes. I do exactly that, and it's just like I was telling you before. \n\nIt’s the same old song and dance every single time. Before I even try to leave the house for one of those loan meetings with the bank, or before my wife makes me go sit behind her craft-fair table for the afternoon, I get completely stuck in this... this loop. I'll be staring into the bathroom mirror, just fixated, and then if I finally manage to break away and get outside, I'm right back at it in the truck mirror. I'll sit there with the engine running, pulling the visor down, turning that old green hat of mine this way and that, adjusting the brim a fraction of an inch at a time, just praying it hides the worst of my sagging cheeks. \n\nAnd the mental part of it, like you asked about, thinking about how others see me... that's exactly what happens when I'm looking at my hands. I'll be out in the yard, and I just start comparing my hands to the younger workers we hire on for the season. I'll be mentally going over how smooth and strong their skin is, and then looking at my own spotted claws, just obsessing over the difference. I know it doesn't make a lick of sense. I'm a sixty-two-year-old man, I know that turning my hat for the fifteenth time or staring at a twenty-year-old's hands isn't going to fix my skin. \n\nBut I just feel like I have to keep doing it. I keep checking the mirrors, keep fiddling with the hat, keep comparing myself to those young guys, until suddenly I look at the clock and realize I've wasted all this precious time I should be using on the farm. Time I could be out checking the irrigation lines, or fixing a busted fence post, or actually getting to the bank. I've run a farm for decades, I know how to manage a crew and manage my day, but when it comes to this... I just can't seem to walk away from a mirror without losing half my morning to it. It’s baffling, it really is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I really do feel like I'm stuck on a loop today, because I swear we just covered this exact ground, didn't we? I feel like I'm talking in circles here! But I'll spell it out again, because maybe I didn't explain it right the first time. Yes. The answer is absolutely yes, it interferes with my ability to function, and it brings my whole day to a grinding halt. 

Like I was saying just a few minutes ago about the farm... I have literally missed scheduled, important calls with our bankers. And in this economy, you don't just dodge the bank, you know? But I end up putting off these massive, critical farm decisions—decisions about equipment, about financing for the next season—all because I get so completely paralyzed by how I look. I'll be in the house, trying to get myself looking just acceptable enough to go sit down in a room with these people, and I just can't do it. The anxiety just eats me alive until I miss the meeting entirely. I just hide out instead. 

And it's not just the farm business, it's my wife's business too, like I mentioned. I was telling you about her retreat and the craft sales... she works her fingers to the bone building up this agritourism side of things. All she wants is a couple of nice pictures of us to put online to promote the whole thing. People love that stuff, the whole 'authentic farm family' image, it really helps the business. But I've completely stopped agreeing to take any photos. The thought of my sagging cheeks and these spotted, decaying hands being frozen on the internet for everyone to see... it makes me so sick with worry that I just flat out refuse. I know it hurts her sales, I know I'm letting her down, and I know sitting here that it's probably excessive, like you said. I know I'm a sixty-two-year-old farmer and I'm supposed to look a little weathered. But in the moment? I just can't function past it. It just completely runs my life, doc.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit hard to explain without sounding foolish, I suppose. I mean, I'm 62 years old, I've worked the land my whole life, been out in the sun, the wind, the dirt. You expect some wear and tear, right? But lately, I just... I can't get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. It's not just a passing thought, it's like this heavy thing sitting on my chest all day. I look in the mirror and all I see are these deep, jagged cracks in my skin, these dark sun spots that seem to be multiplying, and the way my cheeks are just sagging down. It's like my face is melting off my skull. \n\nAnd it's not just when I'm at home. It's exhausting, doc, it really is. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like working the land or being around family?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's an interesting question, because I've been trying to figure that out myself. You see, the land, this farm... it's been my whole life. I took it over from my dad when I was just twenty-two, right after I did a couple years at the community college learning about soil science and agribusiness, thinking I was going to modernize the whole operation.
